\[2^3 - 1^3 + 4^3 - 3^3 + 6^3 - 5^3 + \dots + 18^3 - 17^3?\]

content: {"reasoning": "Let's denote the number of left-handed players as L and the number of right-handed players as R. According to the problem, R = 2L. Therefore, the total number of players participating in the tournament is L + R = L + 2L = 3L. Each player plays every other player exactly once, so the total number of games played is the combination C(n, 2) where n is the total number of players. Thus, the total number of games played is (3L * (3L - 1)) / 2. It is also given that the number of games won by left-handed players is 40% more than those won by right-handed players. Let W_L and W_R represent the games won by left-handed and right-handed players, respectively. Therefore, W_L = 1.4 * W_R. Since there are no ties, W_L + W_R = total games played. Define the equation: W_L = 1.4W_R and W_L + W_R = (3L * (3L - 1)) / 2. Solving these equations simultaneously, we get W_R = (3L * (3L - 1)) / 4.2. Since W_R must be an integer, (3L * (3L - 1)) must be divisible by 4.2. Using trial and error, when L = 7, the number of right-handed players R becomes 14. Thus the total number of players is 21. Therefore total games played is (21 * 20) / 2 = 210. ", "answer": 210}

  "reasoning": "Let's denote the number of left-handed players by L and the number of right-handed players by R. content: {
  "reasoning": "To solve the problem, let's denote the number of left-handed players by L and the number of right-handed players by R. We know from the problem description that R = 2L. Hence, the total number of players P can be expressed as P = L + R = L + 2L = 3L.\n\nIn any round-robin tournament where each participant plays against each other exactly once, the total number of games played is determined by the combination formula C(P, 2) which calculates the number of ways to choose 2 participants from P to play a game. This is given by P * (P - 1) / 2.\n\nWe are given that the number of games won by left-handed players is 40% more than the number of games won by right-handed players. Let's denote the number of games won by right-handed players as W_R and by left-handed players as W_L. Then, W_L = W_R + 0.4 * W_R = 1.4 * W_R.\n\nSince every game results in one win and all winners are accounted for either in the left-handed or right-handed players' wins, the total number of games won is equivalent to the total number of games played: W_R + W_L = Total games played.\n\nSetting up the equation for games won:\nW_R + 1.4 * W_R = Total games,\n2.4 * W_R = Total games.\n\nThus, we need to relate this to the total number of games based on the number of participants:\nTotal games = P * (P - 1) / 2,\n2.4 * W_R = 3L * (3L - 1) / 2.\n\nSolving for W_R requires some algebra but simplifies to finding the integer L that fits the conditions. Substituting P = 3L into the games equation above gives the quadratic:\n2.4W_R = 9L^2 - 3L / 2.\n\nAlthough tedious to solve directly, assuming W_R is an integer and fitting the simplified equation, we find it practical to test small values of L. After several iterations, using trial and checking consistency, the plausible number confirming both conditions (twice of L and the winning ratio) and fitting an integer W_R falls at L = 6. Hence P = 18.\n\nIn conclusion, substituting back, the number of total games played with 18 players (P = 18):\nGames = 18 * 17 / 2 = 153.",
  "answer": 153

content: {
  "reasoning": "Let the number of left-handed players be L, then the number of right-handed players is 2L. The total number of participants is L + 2L = 3L. Each participant plays with every other participant exactly once, so the total number of games played is the number of ways to choose 2 participants out of 3L, which is given by the combination formula C(n, 2) = n(n - 1)/2.\n\nTherefore, the total number of games played is:\n\nC(3L, 2) = (3L * (3L - 1))/2.\n\nThe number of games won by left-handed players is 1.4 times the number of games won by right-handed players. Let the number of games won by right-handed players be R. Then, the number of games won by left-handed players is 1.4R. Thus, the total games won is:\n\nR + 1.4R = 2.4R.\n\nSince every game has a winner and a loser, the total number of games played is also equal to the total number of games won:\n\n(3L(3L - 1))/2 = 2.4R.\n\nThe number of games can be divided into those played by left-handed players against right-handed players, those played between left-handed players only, and those played between right-handed players only.\n\nThe games between left-handed players are C(L, 2) = L * (L - 1) / 2, and the games between right-handed players are C(2L, 2) = (2L * (2L - 1)) / 2. The games played by left-handed against right-handed players are 2L * L.\n\nThus:\n\n(L(L - 1)/2) + ((2L(2L - 1))/2) + 2L * L = 2.4R.\n\nSimplifying: \n\nL^2 - L/2 + 2L^2 - L = R.\n\n3L^2 - L -3L = 2.4R.\n\nSolving for L in terms of R: \n\nWe rearrange the equation:\n\nR = (3L^2 - L - 3L).\n\nNow solving for the number of games: we need to make an assumption:\n- Let's assume L = 5, then games won:\n- The games between left-handed players are 5(5-1)/2 = 10 \n- Games between right-handed players are ((2*5)(2*5-1))/2 = 45 \nThus the games for one wins for each type:\n- 1.4R = 2L * L = 2 * 5 * 5 = 50 => 1.4R = 50 => R = 50 / 1.4 => R = 35.714 games, adjusted for integral, thus R could actually be integer only if L is rightly divisible accordingly, leading approximation derived from conditional assumptions."

문제 {total}:  In a table tennis tournament every participant played every other participant exactly once. Although there were twice as many right-handed players as left-handed players, the number of games won by left-handed players was $40\%$ more than the number of games won by right-handed players. (There were no ties and no ambidextrous players.) What is the total number of games played?

content: {"reasoning": "Let z be a complex number of the form z = x + yi, where x, y are real numbers and i is the imaginary unit. The conjugate of z, denoted as \\( \\overline{z} \\), is x - yi. We need to find the number of complex solutions z such that \\( z^5 = \\overline{z} \\). This equation can be rewritten as: \n\n (x + yi)^5 = x - yi.\n\n A complex number is equal to its conjugate if and only if it is a real number, so one potential solution is when z is a real number. In this scenario, \\( z^5 = z \\). Hence, z could be one of the 5th roots of unity or 0, i.e., solutions of the equation \\( z^4 = 1 \\). These solutions are: \\( z = 0, 1, -1, i, -i \\). \n\n However, we also need to consider solutions where z is not purely real. To explore the situation where z may have a non-zero imaginary part, note that if \\( z^5 = \\overline{z} \\), their magnitudes must also be equal because magnitudes of z raised to any power is also a real number. The magnitude condition implies \n\n |z|^5 = |\\overline{z}| = |z|.\n\n This yields |z|^4 = 1, which implies that |z| = 1. Therefore, any complex solution is on the unit circle.\n\n Plugging into the equation \\( (x + yi)^5 = x - yi \\), with \\( |x + yi| = 1 \\), we will use polar form \\( z = e^{i\\theta} \\). The equation becomes:\n\n (e^{i\\theta})^5 = e^{-i\\theta}.\n\n Therefore, we have:\n\n e^{5i\\theta} = e^{-i\\theta}.\n\n Simplifying gives 6i\\theta = i\\theta + 2k\\pi, or 5i\\theta = 2k\\pi, for some integer k. Which gives \\( \\theta = \\frac{2k\\pi}{5} \\).\n\n For the principal values of θ, we use k = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, which result in five unique solutions, indicating 5 possible distinct angles satisfying this condition.\n\n Another solution includes z being zero, as the equation does not prevent this, adding one more solution (since if z=0, both sides hold true trivially). Combined, these cases yield 6 solutions: Five complex unit circle solutions plus the zero value.\n\n Therefore, the total number of distinct complex numbers satisfying the given equation is 6.",
"answer": 6}

This is an example of a random walk problem on the integer number line where each move is forward and the probability of a move of length 'm' is \\( \\frac{1}{2^m} \\). The idea is to construct a recursive equation for the probability \\( p(n) \\) that Flora reaches position \\( n \\) given that she starts at 0. We note that once she reaches some position \\( n \\), she never moves backward. Therefore, \\( p(0) = 1 \\) because Flora starts at position 0.\n\nThe recursion formula follows:\n\\[ p(n) = \\sum_{m=1}^{\\infty} \\frac{p(n-m)}{2^m} \\]\nThis infinite series expresses \\( p(n) \\) as the sum of probabilities over all possible past positions, weighted by the probability \\( \\frac{1}{2^m} \\) for landing from position \\( n-m \\) to position \\( n \\).\nWe need to evaluate \\( p(10) \\).\nAssuming \\( p(10) = x \\), then:\n\\[ x = \\sum_{m=1}^{9} \\frac{p(10-m)}{2^m} + \\frac{x}{2^{10}} \\]\nNote that \\( p(0) = 1 \\) and for \\( 1 \\leq n \\leq 9, p(n) \\) must be computed same way recursively. If we manually experiment or use a coincidental pattern, it appears that this leads us to conclude \\( p(10) = \\frac{1}{10} \\), which will be a part of the normalized solutions or expected recurrence solutions by structural properties.\nThus the final probability is \\( \\frac{1}{10} \\), and thus \\( m+n = 1+10 = 11\\).",

content: {
  "reasoning": "This problem can be approached by considering a generating function for Flora's position. Let's denote P(x) as the probability generating function for Flora's position, starting at 0. The probability that Flora lands at position x after k jumps can be expressed in terms of the probability P(x). Thus,\n- 3 1/2 cups of orange slices weigh (3.5 * 1/4) = 7/8 pounds.\n- 1/2 cup of orange slices weighs (1/2 * 1/4) = 1/8 pounds.\n\nAccording to the problem, the weight of 1/3 of a large pizza and 3.5 cups of orange slices equals the weight of 3/4 of a large pizza and 0.5 cups of orange slices:\n\n(1/3)P + 7/8 = (3/4)P + 1/8\n\nSubtract (1/3)P from both sides:\n7/8 = (3/4)P - (1/3)P + 1/8\n\nCombine P parts as follows:\n7/8 = [(3/4) - (1/3)]P + 1/8\n\nFind a common denominator for the fractions 3/4 and 1/3:\n3/4 = 9/12 and 1/3 = 4/12, so:\n(3/4) - (1/3) = (9/12) - (4/12) = 5/12\n\nNow, we rewrite the equation:\n7/8 = (5/12)P + 1/8\n\nSubtract 1/8 from both sides to isolate the P term:\n7/8 - 1/8 = (5/12)P\n\n6/8 = (5/12)P\n\nSimplify 6/8:\n3/4 = (5/12)P\n\nMultiply both sides by 12 to solve for P:\n(3/4) * 12 = 5P\n9 = 5P\n\nTherefore, P = 9/5.\n\nThe weight of a large pizza is 9/5 pounds.\n\nTo find the final answer, calculate m-n where P = 9/5:\nm = 9, n = 5, so m-n = 9 - 5 = 4.",

content: {"reasoning": "To solve this problem, we begin by understanding the structure of a regular icosahedron, which has 12 vertices. We need to determine the probability that $d(Q, R) > d(R, S)$ for randomly chosen distinct vertices $Q$, $R$, and $S$.\n\nFirstly, since a regular icosahedron has 12 vertices, and each vertex is directly connected to 5 neighboring vertices (which makes the face of the triangles), the distance between any two adjacent vertices is $d = 1$. If vertices are not directly connected, more edges are needed.\n\nThe farthest distance between any two vertices isn't more than 3, being $d = 3$ the diameter of the regular icosahedron, because one can at most go through a path from a vertex, to the opposite vertex (3 steps).\n\nSince we look at vertices $Q$, $R$, and $S$, where $d(Q, R)$ is the distance between $Q$ and $R$, and $d(R, S)$ is the distance between $R$ and $S$, we analyze cases:\n\n1. When $d(Q,R) = 1$, for $d(Q,R) > d(R,S)$, we need $d(R,S) = 0$, but since $R \\neq S$, this case doesn't exist.\n2. When $d(Q,R) = 2$, for $d(Q,R) > d(R,S)$, $d(R,S)$ must be 1.\n3. When $d(Q,R) = 3$, for $d(Q,R) > d(R,S)$, $d(R,S)$ can be 1 or 2.\n\nWe compute the probability by counting the favorable outcomes over the total cases:\n- There are ${12 \\choose 3}$ ways to pick $(Q, R, S)$ and for any position of $R$, there are $5$ choices for $Q$ since $Q$ should be neighbor to $R$, then for each $Q$-R pair, we are left with 5 possible choices, thus, the overall selectiveness:\n\nTotal Valid Setup Ways: $12 \\cdot 5 \\cdot 5 = 300$. content: {"reasoning": "The regular icosahedron is a polyhedron with 12 vertices and 30 edges. Each vertex is connected to 5 other vertices by edges, and the shortest path between two vertices (the graph distance) is among these direct connections or through intermediate vertices.\n\nOur goal is to find the probability that the distance from vertex Q to R is greater than from R to S, i.e., d(Q,R) > d(R,S). First, consider the possible distances between pairs of vertices:\n1. **Direct neighbors (adjacent vertices):** d(A, B) = 1. With its 5 neighbors, vertex R can only have 5 paths of length 1.\n2. **Vertices separated by a vertex (second neighbors):** d(A, B) = 2. A vertex on an icosahedron has 5 second neighbors at a graph distance of 2. Thus, there are 5 vertices that are two steps away from any given vertex.\n3. **Vertices three edges away (third neighbors):** d(A, B) = 3. 5 of the remaining vertices are 3 steps away, forming the farthest distance possible in this structure due to the constraints of the shape.\n\nSince Q, R, and S are distinct vertices of a regular icosahedron, each with specific properties and considerations in a symmetric layout:\n- For a fixed R, when comparing distances to Q and S, both Q and S can individually assume values with graph distances 1, 2, or 3 from R. We aim to find the probability that the minimum distance d(Q, R) between Q and R is greater than the minimum distance d(R, S) between R and S.\n\nFirst, calculate the total number of ways to choose Q, R, and S:\nWe choose 3 vertices out of 12, so there are:\n\\[ \\binom{12}{3} = 220 \\]\n\nNext, calculate the number of ways where d(Q, R) > d(R, S):\n\n- When d(R, S) = 1, d(Q, R) can be 2 or 3.\n  - For R, S directly connected (1 edge), possible distinct Q such that d(Q, R) > d(R, S):\n    - Q is not adjacent to either R or S (i.e., Q is distant).\n    - Choose Q such that it is not adjacent to either R or S:\n      For each edge (R, S), there are 5 vertices neither adjacent to R nor S, and among them, 4 are at distance 2 and 1 at distance 3.\n    - Therefore, the number of ways is 30(edge choices for R, S) * 5 ways to choose Q = 150.\n\n- When d(R, S) = 2, d(Q, R) = 3 (since Q should not be adjacent to R):\n  - Possible distinct Q that is not on the edge with R and not adjacent to either.\n  - For each such R, S pair, choosing Q non-adjacent is only possible by choosing one of the farthest non-adjacent vertices.\n  - calculate:\n      For 2-edge distinct R, S pairing, a single non-adjacent choice for Q, done for consistent distance 2 edges, 30 expected combination contributions.\n\nSince each distinct selection needs verification for distance consistency, repeat calculations iteratively:\n\nAccumulating 150 from distance 1 to 2, and repeating 30 from distance 2 choices consistent.\n150 + 30 = 180.\n\nProbability:\n\\[ \\frac{180}{220}.\\]\n\nReducing the fraction: (Cancel common factors with simplifying)\n\\[ \\frac{9}{11}.\\]\n\nHence, m + n is obtained simplifying m=9, n=11.\n\\[ m+n = 9+11 = 20. \\]", "answer": 20}

문제 {total}:  If $A$ and $B$ are vertices of a polyhedron, define the distance $d(A,B)$ to be the minimum number of edges of the polyhedron one must traverse in order to connect $A$ and $B$. For example, if $\overline{AB}$ is an edge of the polyhedron, then $d(A, B) = 1$, but if $\overline{AC}$ and $\overline{CB}$ are edges and $\overline{AB}$ is not an edge, then $d(A, B) = 2$. Let $Q$, $R$, and $S$ be randomly chosen distinct vertices of a regular icosahedron (regular polyhedron made up of 20 equilateral triangles). Find the probability that $d(Q, R) > d(R, S)$. The final answer can be written in the form $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. What is $m+n$?
모델 답변: 5
실제 정답: 29

The function f satisfies: \\[ \\sum_{d \\mid n} d \\cdot f \\left( \\frac{n}{d} \\right) = 1 \\] for every positive integer n. This equation is very related to divisor sums and the Möbius inversion formula. We need to determine the value of the function f for a specific integer, f(2023).\n\nTo solve this, let's substitute n with specific values to identify the nature of f.\n\nWhen n = 1, the equation simplifies to: \\[ \\sum_{d \\mid 1} d \\cdot f \\left( \\frac{1}{d} \\right) = 1. \\] Since the only divisor of 1 is 1, it becomes: \\[ 1 \\cdot f(1) = 1, \\] which implies f(1) = 1.\n\nThe function f is determined recursively; f(n) can be expressed using its values at smaller divisors through the Möbius function:\n\nMöbius inversion gives a formula relating the two functions for divisor sums:\n\\[ f(n) = \\sum_{d \\mid n} \\mu(d) \\cdot \\frac{1}{d}, \\] where \\( \\mu \\) is the Möbius function.\n\nFor f(2023), we compute the sum of \\( \\mu(d) \\cdot \\frac{1}{d} \\) over all divisors d of 2023.\n\nTo do this, first find the prime factorization of 2023. content: {
  "reasoning": "To solve this problem, we need to find the number of sequences where each subset is a subset of the next. 문제 {total}:  Janet rolls a standard $6$-sided die $4$ times and keeps a running total of the numbers she rolls. What is the probability that at some point, her running total will equal $3$? The final answer can be written in the form $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. What is $m+n$?

content: {
  "reasoning": "Consider the coordinates of points A and B on the curve. Let A be (a, log2(a)) and B be (b, log2(b)). The midpoint of segment AB is given as (6, 2). Therefore, the coordinates for the midpoint, which is the average of the coordinates of A and B, can be represented by the equations (a + b)/2 = 6 and (log2(a) + log2(b))/2 = 2. Solving these equations gives us the system: \n\n1. a + b = 12 \n2. log2(a) + log2(b) = 4 \n\nFrom the second equation, log2(ab) = 4, meaning ab = 2^4 = 16. Now we have the system of equations: \n\n1. a + b = 12 \n2. ab = 16 \n\nThese are the sum and product of the roots of the quadratic equation x^2 - 12x + 16 = 0. Solving for x using the quadratic formula gives: \n\nx = [12 ± sqrt(12^2 - 4 * 1 * 16)] / 2 \n  = [12 ± sqrt(144 - 64)] / 2 \n  = [12 ± sqrt(80)] / 2 \n  = [12 ± 4√5] / 2 \n\nThus, the roots are 6 ± 2√5, giving us the x-coordinates of A and B as 6 + 2√5 and 6 - 2√5, respectively. The positive difference between these x-coordinates is: \n\n(6 + 2√5) - (6 - 2√5) = 4√5 \n\nThis difference is in form m√n, with m=4 and n=5. Therefore, m + n = 4 + 5 = 9.",
  "answer": 9

content: {
  "reasoning": "We know the midpoint formula for two points \\( (x_1, y_1) \\) and \\( (x_2, y_2) \\) is \\( \\left( \\frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \\frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \\right) \\). Given that the midpoint is \\( (6, 2) \\), we thus have: \\[ \\frac{x_1 + x_2}{2} = 6 \\] \\[ \\frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} = 2 \\] Simplifying them, we have \\( x_1 + x_2 = 12 \\) and \\( y_1 + y_2 = 4 \\). Since the points \\( A(x_1, y_1) \\) and \\( B(x_2, y_2) \\) lie on the graph of \\( y = \\log_2(x) \\), we have \\( y_1 = \\log_2(x_1) \\) and \\( y_2 = \\log_2(x_2) \\).   "reasoning": "Mrs. Jones has poured orange juice into three glasses such that each is full, and the fourth glass only has \\( \\frac{1}{3} \\) of juice in it. content: {
  "reasoning": "Let the capacity of each glass be denoted as C. Mrs. Jones fills the first three glasses completely, which means each glass has C amount of juice. The fourth glass only contains C/3 of juice. To equalize the juice in all four glasses, we need to look at the total amount of juice first: 3C + C/3. The next step is to distribute this juice equally among the four glasses. \n\nThe total amount of juice is 3C + C/3 = (9C + C) / 3 = 10C / 3. The amount of juice each glass should have is (10C / 3) / 4 = 10C / 12 = 5C / 6. \n\nNow, we find how much juice needs to be taken out of each of the first three glasses. Each of these glasses originally has C juice, so we need to pour (C - 5C/6) from each of the first three glasses. \n\nCalculating this, C - 5C/6 = 6C/6 - 5C/6 = C/6. \n\nThus, Mrs. Jones will pour C/6 of juice from each of the first three glasses to the fourth glass. For all glasses to have the same amount of juice, the fraction of juice to pour from each glass into the fourth glass is 1/6. In terms of m and n, this is 1 and 6 respectively, so m+n = 1+6 = 7.",
  "answer": 7

content: {"reasoning": "Mrs. Jones has four identical glasses. The first three glasses are full, and the fourth glass is 1/3 full. We want all four glasses to have the same amount of juice. Let's denote the full capacity of a glass as 1 unit. The total amount of juice in all four glasses is 3 + 1/3 = 10/3 units. Since we want each glass to have the same amount of juice, we need to divide the total juice equally among the four glasses. Thus, each glass should have (10/3) / 4 = 10/12 = 5/6 of a glass of juice. The first three glasses are currently full (1 unit each), and they need to have 5/6 units each, so we need to pour out 1 - 5/6 = 1/6 of a glass from each of these three glasses. The fourth glass is currently 1/3 full and needs to reach 5/6 full, so it needs an additional 5/6 - 1/3 = 5/6 - 2/6 = 3/6 = 1/2 unit. To get 1/2 unit in the fourth glass, we pour 1/6 unit from each of the first three glasses (3 * 1/6 = 3/6 = 1/2). Therefore, the correct fraction of a glass to pour from each of the first three glasses into the fourth glass is 1/6. In simplest terms, m = 1 and n = 6 so m + n = 7.", "answer": 7}

    "reasoning": "Mrs. Jones has four glasses and pours orange juice into each. The first three glasses are full while the fourth is only 1/3 full. To equalize the amount in each glass, Mrs. Jones redistributes the juice. Let's denote the total capacity of each full glass as C. Therefore, the total juice in the first three glasses is 3C, and the juice in the fourth glass is C/3. The total amount of juice across the four glasses is 3C + C/3. To find the equal amount of juice that should be in each glass (denoted as F), we need: \n\nF = (Total juice)/(Number of glasses) = (3C + C/3)/4 = (9C/3 + C/3)/4 = 10C/3 ÷ 4 = 10C/12 = 5C/6\n\nEach full glass initially contains C. To achieve F = 5C/6 in each glass, we need to transfer (C - 5C/6) = C/6 from each of the first three glasses. \n\nThe amount of juice transferred to the fourth glass is 3 * C/6 = C/2, bringing it from C/3 to C/3 + C/2 = (2C/6 + 3C/6) = 5C/6, matching the amount in each of the other glasses. \n\nThus, the fraction of a glass Mrs. Jones needs to pour from each of the first three glasses is C/6. \n\nExpressed in the form m/n, this is 1/6, where m = 1 and n = 6. Therefore, m+n = 1+6 = 7.",
    "answer": 7

문제 {total}:  Mrs. Jones is pouring orange juice into four identical glasses for her four sons. She fills the first three glasses completely but runs out of juice when the fourth glass is only $\frac{1}{3}$ full. What fraction of a glass must Mrs. Jones pour from each of the first three glasses into the fourth glass so that all four glasses will have the same amount of juice? The final answer can be written in the form $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. What is $m+n$?
모델 답변: 7
실제 정답: 7

  "reasoning": "To solve the problem, we first need to determine the locations of the centers of the two circles. The first circle has a radius of 4 and its center is on the positive x-axis. Since it is tangent to the y-axis at the origin, the distance from the origin to the center is also 4 units, making the center of this circle (4, 0).\n\nThe second circle has a radius of 10 and its center is on the positive y-axis. It is tangent to the x-axis at the origin, thus the distance from the origin to the center is 10 units, resulting in the center of this circle being (0, 10).\n\nNext, we need to find the points of intersection of these two circles. The equation of the first circle is:\n (x - 4)^2 + y^2 = 16.\nThe equation of the second circle is:\n x^2 + (y - 10)^2 = 100.\n\nTo find their intersection points, we solve these equations simultaneously. Expanding both equations, we get:\n 1) x^2 - 8x + 16 + y^2 = 16  ---->  x^2 + y^2 = 8x  \n 2) x^2 + y^2 - 20y + 100 = 100 ----> x^2 + y^2 = 20y.\n\nEquating the expressions for x^2 + y^2 from the two equations, we have:\n 8x = 20y.\nThis implies y = 0.4x.\nSubstituting y = 0.4x in the first circle's equation x^2 + y^2 = 8x, we get:\n x^2 + (0.4x)^2 = 8x => x^2 + 0.16x^2 = 8x => 1.16x^2 - 8x = 0.\nFactoring out x, we have x(1.16x - 8) = 0. Ignoring the trivial solution x = 0, we have 1.16x = 8; therefore, x = 6.8965.\nNow using y = 0.4x, we find y = 0.4(6.8965) = 2.7586.\n\nTo find the second intersection point, notice that the circles are symmetrical about the line y = 0.4x due to their equations' forms. Thus, the second point of intersection is the mirror, i.e., (-6.8965, -2.7586).\n\nNow, we find the slope of the line passing through these intersection points:\n m = (y2 - y1) / (x2 - x1) = (-2.7586 - 2.7586) / (-6.8965 - 6.8965),\n resulting in m = (-5.5172) / (-13.793) = 0.4.\n\nThe slope, 0.4, can be expressed as m/n = 2/5. Therefore, if m/n = 2/5, then m + n = 2 + 5.\n",

  "reasoning": "To find the centers of the circles, we begin by analyzing their tangency conditions. For the circle of radius 4 tangent to the y-axis at the origin, the center must be located at (4, 0) since the center is on the positive x-axis and the radius is 4. Similarly, for the circle of radius 10 tangent to the x-axis at the origin, the center must be located at (0, 10) since the center is on the positive y-axis.\n\nThe equation of the first circle with center (4, 0) is:\n\\[(x - 4)^2 + y^2 = 4^2 = 16.\\]\nThe equation of the second circle with center (0, 10) is:\n\\[x^2 + (y - 10)^2 = 10^2 = 100.\\]\n\nWe solve these two equations simultaneously to find the intersection points of these two circles.\n\n1. Substitute y from the first circle equation into the second equation.   "reasoning": "Given the complex number $z = x + yi$, where $x$ and $y$ are real numbers, we will compute $z \times z$ and equate it to $z^2 + 40$ based on the operation definition provided. By definition, given $u = a + bi$ and $v = c + di$, the operation $u \times v = ac + bdi$. For $z \times z$, we have $u = z = x + yi$ and $v = z = x + yi$. Comparing real parts and imaginary parts separately gives: $x^2 = x^2 - y^2 + 40$ and $y^2 = 2xy$. Substituting $y = 0$ satisfies the imaginary part equation, then real part gives $x^2 = x^2 + 40$, which is a contradiction, hence $y$ must not be $0$. Solving $y^2 = 2xy$, we get $y = 0$ (from above) or $y = 2x$ (assuming $x$ and $y$ are nonzero). Substituting $y = 2x$ in the real part condition $x^2 = x^2 - (2x)^2 + 40$, which becomes $0 = -4x^2 + 40$, solving gives $4x^2 = 40$, resulting in $x^2 = 10$. Then $|z|^2 = x^2 + y^2 = x^2 + (2x)^2 = x^2 + 4x^2 = 5x^2 = 5(10) = 50$.\nTherefore, $|z|^2$ is 50.",

$u \otimes v = ac + bdi$
Suppose $z$ is a complex number such that $z\otimes z = z^{2}+40$. What is $|z|^2$?

  "reasoning": "We have a rectangular box with distinct edge lengths $a$, $b$, and $c$. The problem gives us three conditions:\n\n1. The sum of the areas of all 6 faces is \\( \\frac{11}{2} \\):\n   \\[ 2(ab + bc + ca) = \\frac{11}{2}. \\]\n   Dividing both sides by 2 gives us: \n   \\[ ab + bc + ca = \\frac{11}{4}. \\]\n\n3. The volume of the box is \\( \\frac{1}{2} \\):\n   \\[ abc = \\frac{1}{2}. \\]\n\nTo find the longest interior diagonal, we use the formula:\n   \\[ \\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}. \\]\n\nWe know from the identity:\n   \\[ (a + b + c)^2 = a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + 2(ab + bc + ca). \\]\n\nPlugging the known sums into this identity:\n   \\[ \\left( \\frac{13}{4} \\right)^2 = a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + 2 \\times \\frac{11}{4}. \\]\n   \\[ \\frac{169}{16} = a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + \\frac{22}{4}. \\]\n   Simplifying \\( \\frac{22}{4} \\) to \\( \\frac{44}{16} \\), then subtracting from \\( \\frac{169}{16} \\):\n   \\[ a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = \\frac{169}{16} - \\frac{44}{16} = \\frac{125}{16}. \\]\n\nFinally, the longest interior diagonal is:\n   \\[ \\sqrt{\\frac{125}{16}} = \\frac{\\sqrt{125}}{4}. \\]\n   Simplifying the square root gives: \\(  \\sqrt{125} = \\sqrt{5^3} = 5 \\sqrt{5} \\).\n   Thus, the diagonal length is \\( \\frac{5 \\sqrt{5}}{4} \\), which can be written as \\( \\frac{5 \\times \\sqrt{5}}{4} \\). The volume of the box is \\( \frac{1}{2} \\), i.e., \\( abc = \frac{1}{2} \\).\n\nTo find the longest interior diagonal, we need \\( \\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2} \\). From \\( (a+b+c)^2 = a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + 2(ab + bc + ca) \\):\n- \\( \\left(\\frac{13}{4}\\right)^2 = a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + 2\\left(\\frac{11}{4}\\right) \\)\n- So \\( \\frac{169}{16} = a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + \\frac{11}{2} \\).\n- \\( a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = \\frac{169}{16} - \\frac{22}{4} = \\frac{169}{16} - \\frac{88}{16} = \\frac{81}{16} \\).\nThus, the length of the longest interior diagonal is \\( \\sqrt{\\frac{81}{16}} = \\frac{9}{4} \\).\nSince this is in the form \\( \\frac{m}{n} \\), we have \\( m = 9 \\) and \\( n = 4 \\).   "reasoning": "The coin values in Coinland are 6, 10, and 15 cents. To determine the largest amount of money that cannot be formed using these coins, we use a known result from number theory when dealing with multiple coins. Given that the smallest side $a = 6$, we have the sides as $6$, $6 + d$, and $6 + 2d$.\n\nSince the triangle has an angle measuring $120^\\circ$, assume $\\angle C = 120^\\circ$, so we can apply the Law of Cosines:\n\n$c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab \\cos(120^\\circ).$\n\nBecause $\\cos(120^\\circ) = -\\frac{1}{2}$, the formula becomes:\n\n$(6 + 2d)^2 = 6^2 + (6 + d)^2 + 6 \\cdot (6 + d) \\cdot 1/2.$\n\nSimplifying, we have:\n$(6 + 2d)^2 = 36 + (36 + 12d + d^2) + 18 + 3d.$\n\nExpanding and equating both sides yields:\n$36 + 24d + 4d^2 = 90 + 15d + d^2,$\n\ntherefore:\n$4d^2 + 24d + 36 = 90 + 15d + d^2.$\n\nSimplify and rearrange terms:\n$4d^2 + 24d + 36 = 90 + 15d + d^2,$ which simplifies to:\n$3d^2 + 9d - 54 = 0.$\n\nDividing the entire equation by 3:\n$d^2 + 3d - 18 = 0.$\n\nThis is a quadratic equation, solving using factorization:\n$(d + 6)(d - 3) = 0.$\n\nSo we have solutions $d = -6$ and $d = 3$. Since $d$ must be positive, $d = 3$.\n\nTherefore, side lengths are $6$, $9$, and $12$.\n\nLet's calculate the area using the sine area formula, since we know angle $C$ is $120^\\circ$:\n \nArea $= \\frac{1}{2}ab \\sin C$, where $a = 6$ and $b = 9$:\n\nArea $= \\frac{1}{2} \\times 6 \\times 9 \\times \\sin(120^\\circ).$\n\nWe know $\\sin(120^\\circ) = \\sin(60^\\circ) = \\frac{\\sqrt{3}}{2}$, thus:\n\nArea $= \\frac{1}{2} \\times 6 \\times 9 \\times \\frac{\\sqrt{3}}{2} = \\frac{27\\sqrt{3}}{2}$.\n\nHence, the area of the triangle is $27\\sqrt{3}/2$.\n\nThe problem states to simplify the expression to $m\\sqrt{n}$ form, and find $m+n$. Here, we have $m = \\frac{27}{2}$ and $n = 3$. Calculating $m + n$ gives us:\n\n$\\frac{27}{2}$ is equivalent to keeping the expression in terms of whole numbers as $27 \\sqrt{3} / 2$, but simplification to $m \\sqrt{n}$ requires integer values of $m$ which cannot be divided by 2 in this form. \n\nAlternately, consider $m = 27$, $n = 3$, providing integer values of $m$, with $n$ already simplifying within the square root factor condition.\n\nTherefore, the correct simplification using integer $m = 27$, $n = 3$, $m+n = 30$.", "answer": 30}

문제 {total}:  Suppose that $a$, $b$, $c$ and $d$ are positive integers satisfying all of the following relations.
\[abcd=2^6\cdot 3^9\cdot 5^7\]
\[\text{lcm}(a,b)=2^3\cdot 3^2\cdot 5^3\]
\[\text{lcm}(a,c)=2^3\cdot 3^3\cdot 5^3\]
\[\text{lcm}(a,d)=2^3\cdot 3^3\cdot 5^3\]
\[\text{lcm}(b,c)=2^1\cdot 3^3\cdot 5^2\]
\[\text{lcm}(b,d)=2^2\cdot 3^3\cdot 5^2\]
\[\text{lcm}(c,d)=2^2\cdot 3^3\cdot 5^2\]
What is $\text{gcd}(a,b,c,d)$?
모델 답변: 1
실제 정답: 3
--------------------------------------------------
content: {
  "reasoning": "A right triangle's hypotenuse is the diameter of the circumscribed circle. For the $3-4-5$ triangle inscribed in circle $A$, the hypotenuse is 5. Thus, the diameter of circle $A$ is 5, and its radius is 5/2. The area of circle $A$ is \\( \\pi \\left( \\frac{5}{2} \\right)^2 = \\frac{25\\pi}{4} \\). \n\nFor the $5-12-13$ triangle inscribed in circle $B$, the hypotenuse is 13. Thus, the diameter of circle $B$ is 13, and its radius is 13/2. The area of circle $B$ is \\( \\pi \\left( \\frac{13}{2} \\right)^2 = \\frac{169\\pi}{4} \\).\n\nThe ratio of the area of circle $A$ to the area of circle $B$ is \\( \\frac{\\frac{25\\pi}{4}}{\\frac{169\\pi}{4}} = \\frac{25\\pi}{4} \\times \\frac{4}{169\\pi} = \\frac{25}{169} \\).\n\nHence, the ratio is \\( \\frac{25}{169} \\) and since 25 and 169 are relatively prime, the final answer is 25 + 169 = 194.",
  "answer": 194

content: {
  "reasoning": "When a right triangle is inscribed in a circle, the hypotenuse of the triangle is equal to the diameter of the circle. The area of a circle is determined by the formula \\( \\pi r^2 \\), where \\( r \\) is the radius of the circle.\n\nFor circle A, the triangle inscribed is a 3-4-5 right triangle. Hence, the hypotenuse is 5, which is the diameter of circle A. Therefore, the radius of circle A is \\( \\frac{5}{2} \\). The area of circle A is:\n\\[ \\text{Area of circle A} = \\pi \\left(\\frac{5}{2}\\right)^2 = \\pi \\times \\frac{25}{4}. \\]\n\nFor circle B, the triangle inscribed is a 5-12-13 right triangle. Hence, the hypotenuse is 13, which is the diameter of circle B. Therefore, the radius of circle B is \\( \\frac{13}{2} \\). Therefore, \\(m+n = 25 + 169 = 194\\).",

문제 {total}:  You are playing a game. A $2 \times 1$ rectangle covers two adjacent squares (oriented either horizontally or vertically) of a $3 \times 3$ grid of squares, but you are not told which two squares are covered. Your goal is to find at least one square that is covered by the rectangle. A "turn" consists of you guessing a square, after which you are told whether that square is covered by the hidden rectangle. What is the minimum number of turns you need to ensure that at least one of your guessed squares is covered by the rectangle?

문제 {total}:  When the roots of the polynomial 
\[P(x)  = (x-1)^1 (x-2)^2 (x-3)^3 \cdot \cdot \cdot (x-10)^{10}\]
are removed from the number line, what remains is the union of $11$ disjoint open intervals. On how many of these intervals is $P(x)$ positive?

content: {"reasoning": "We begin by analyzing the components of the expression. We have \n\\[\n\\sqrt{\\frac{\\log (n^2) - (\\log n)^2}{\\log n - 3}}.\n\\]\nFirst, rewrite the numerator. Using the logarithmic identity, we have \\( \\log (n^2) = 2\\log n \\). Thus, the numerator becomes:\n\\[\n\\log (n^2) - (\\log n)^2 = 2\\log n - (\\log n)^2 = \\log n (2 - \\log n).\n\\]\nNow, consider the fraction under the square root:\n\\[\n\\frac{\\log n (2 - \\log n)}{\\log n - 3}.\n\\]\nFor the expression to represent a real number, several conditions are necessary:\n1. The logarithm \\( \\log n \\) must be defined, which implies \\( n > 0 \\).\n2. The denominator \\( \\log n - 3 \\neq 0 \\), meaning \\( \\log n \\neq 3 \\) or equivalently \\( n \\neq 10^3 = 1000 \\).\n3. The expression under the square root must be non-negative:\n\\[\n\\log n (2 - \\log n) \\geq 0.\n\\]\nLet's solve the inequality \\( \\log n (2 - \\log n) \\geq 0 \\):\n- If \\( \\log n = 0 \\), then \\( n = 10^0 = 1 \\) and the expression is \\( 0\\), which is non-negative.\n- For \\( \\log n > 0 \\), \\( n > 1 \\) and \\( 2 - \\log n > 0 \\) gives \\( \\log n < 2 \\), or \\( n < 100 \\).\n- For \\( \\log n < 0 \\), \\( 0 < n < 1 \\) and \\( 2 - \\log n > 2 \\), making the product positive.\n\nTherefore, \\( n \\) must satisfy \\( 0 < n < 100 \\) and \\( n \\neq 1000 \\).\nCombining these conditions, the eligible integer values are from \\( n = 1 \\) to \\( n = 99 \\) inclusive.\n\nThus, there are 99 such integers.", "answer": 99}

We need to compute this for each possible k from 1 to 12 (as k=0 gives an impossible scenario of selecting subsets). The final result will be the sum:  C(11, 0) + C(10, 1) + C(9, 2) + ... + C(0, 11). The number of ways to choose $k-1$ elements from $12-k$ is given by the combination $\\binom{12-k}{k-1}$. Therefore, for each possible least element $k$ from 1 to 12 (since $k=0$ would require a 0-element subset, which is empty), the number of valid subsets with least element $k$ is $\\binom{12-k}{k-1}$.   "reasoning": "To solve this problem, we need to determine how many nonempty subsets B of the set S = {0, 1, 2, 3, ..., 12} satisfy the condition that the number of elements in B is equal to the least element of B. \n\nLet's consider each possible value of the least element, denoted as k, where k ranges from 1 to 12 (we exclude 0 since we want nonempty subsets). \n\n- If k is the least element of B, then the subset B must contain exactly k elements.\n- The elements of B, other than the least element k itself, must be chosen from the remaining elements of S, which are {k+1, k+2, ..., 12}.\n\nFor each k, the number of additional elements to choose from is (12 - k). We need to choose (k - 1) elements from these (12 - k) elements, as one element (k itself) is already included.\n\nThe number of ways to choose (k - 1) elements out of (12 - k) is given by the combination formula C(n, r) = n! / [r! * (n-r)!], where n is the total number of available elements to choose from, and r is the number of items to choose.\n\nTherefore, for each k, the number of valid subsets is given by:\n\nNumber of subsets for a fixed least element k = C(12 - k, k - 1).\n\nWe sum this expression over all valid k from 1 to 12:\n\nTotal number of subsets = sum(C(12-k, k-1) for k in range(1, 13)).\n\nCalculating each term for k = 1 to 12, we find:\n\n- k = 1: C(11, 0) = 1\n- k = 2: C(10, 1) = 10\n- k = 3: C(9, 2) = 36\n- k = 4: C(8, 3) = 56\n- k = 5: C(7, 4) = 35\n- k = 6: C(6, 5) = 6\n- k = 7: C(5, 6) = 0\n- k = 8: C(4, 7) = 0\n- k = 9: C(3, 8) = 0\n- k = 10: C(2, 9) = 0\n- k = 11: C(1, 10) = 0\n- k = 12: C(0, 11) = 0\n\nAdding up these values gives:\n1 + 10 + 36 + 56 + 35 + 6 = 144.\n\nNote that for k greater than 6, the number of elements to choose (k - 1) exceeds the available elements (12 - k), hence those terms result in a value of 0.",
  "answer": 144

content: {"reasoning": "We are given a set A = {0, 1, 2, 3, ..., 12}. We need to find the number of nonempty subsets B of A such that the number of elements in B is equal to the least element of B. Let's denote the least element of B as 'k'. For a subset B to satisfy this condition, it must have exactly k elements, and the smallest element must be k. Thus, a subset B that satisfies the condition will look like {k, a1, a2, ..., a_{k-1}}, where k < a1 < a2 < ... < a_{k-1} and ai are elements from the set \\{k+1, k+2, ..., 12\\}. Therefore, the number of such subsets B for a given k is the number of ways to choose the remaining k-1 elements from the set \\{k+1, k+2, ..., 12\\}, which are 12-k elements in total. This is given by the binomial coefficient \\(\\binom{12-k}{k-1}\\). We calculate this for each k from 1 to 12 and then sum the results.\n\nFor k = 1, choose 0 elements from {2, 3, ..., 12}: \\(\\binom{11}{0} = 1\\).\nFor k = 2, choose 1 elements from {3, 4, ..., 12}: \\(\\binom{10}{1} = 10\\).\nFor k = 3, choose 2 elements from {4, 5, ..., 12}: \\(\\binom{9}{2} = 36\\).\nFor k = 4, choose 3 elements from {5, 6, ..., 12}: \\(\\binom{8}{3} = 56\\).\nFor k = 5, choose 4 elements from {6, 7, ..., 12}: \\(\\binom{7}{4} = 35\\).\nFor k = 6, choose 5 elements from {7, 8, ..., 12}: \\(\\binom{6}{5} = 6\\).\nFor k = 7, choose 6 elements from {8, 9, 10, 11, 12}: \\(\\binom{5}{6} = 0\\) (not possible since there are fewer than 6 elements).\nFor k = 8, 9, 10, ... choosing k-1 elements from fewer than 5, 4, 3, etc., elements gives 0 as well, following the same reasoning until k=12.\n\nSumming them yields 1 + 10 + 36 + 56 + 35 + 6 = 144.\n\nTherefore, the total number of nonempty subsets B is 144.","answer": 144}

For \\(x < 0\\) and \\(y < 0\\), the inequality simplifies to \n   \\((-x - 1) + (-y - 1) \\le 1\\) leading to \\(-x - y \\le 3\\).\n4. For \\(x \\ge 0\\) and \\(y < 0\\), the inequality simplifies to \n   \\((x - 1) + (-y - 1) \\le 1\\) leading to \\(x - y \\le 3\\).\n\nThese inequalities form the boundary of a shape (a square with vertices chopped off by the lines they create). Particularly, within these bounds:\n- In Quadrant I: \\((x, y)\\) moves from \\((1, 1)\\) up to \\((2, 1)\\), \\((1, 2)\\)\n- In Quadrant II: moves between \\((-1, 1)\\) up to \\((0, 2)\\)\n- In Quadrant III: moves between \\((-1, -1)\\) to \\((0, -2)\\)\n- In Quadrant IV: moves between \\((1, -1)\\) to \\((2, -2)\\)\n\nThe resultant is a diamond shape with vertices at \\((2, 0), (0, 2), (-2, 0), (0, -2)\\).\n\nTo calculate the area, note that it is a rhombus with diagonals of length 4 each (from (2, 0) to (-2, 0) and from (0, 2) to (0, -2)). The area A of rhombus with diagonals \\(p\\) and \\(q\\) is \\(A = \\frac{1}{2}pq\\).\nPlugging in, \\(A = \\frac{1}{2} \\times 4 \\times 4 = 8\\).",
  "answer": 8
}

$| | x | - 1 | + | | y | - 1 | \le 1$?
